    66	:code:`sepal_ui` is a lib designed to create elegant python based dashboard in the `SEPAL environment <https://sepal.io/>`__. It is designed on top of the amazing `ipyvuetify <https://ipyvuetify.readthedocs.io/en/latest/introduction.html>`_ library and will help developer to easily create interface for their workflows. 
    67	By using this libraries, you'll ensure a robust and unified interface for your scripts and a easy and complete integration into the SEPAL dashboard of application.

tool: ./<#sep#>You are given a list of positive integers `numbers`. Your task is to implement a function `find_largest_product` that computes the largest product of any three distinct numbers from the list. The function should return this maximum product. Assume that the list contains at least three numbers.

**Sample Input:**
```
[1, 10, 2, 6, 5, 3]
```

**Sample Output:**
```
300
```

```python
def find_largest_product(numbers):
    """
    Computes the largest product of any three distinct numbers from the list.

    :param numbers: List of positive integers
    :return: The largest product of any three distinct numbers
    """
    if len(numbers) < 3:
        raise ValueError("List must contain at least three numbers.")
    
    numbers.sort()
    
    return max(numbers[-1] * numbers[-2] * numbers[-3],
               numbers[0] * numbers[1] * numbers[-1])

# Example usage:
numbers = [1, 10, 2, 6, 5, 3]
print(find_largest_product(numbers))  # Output: 300
```<#sep#>(x) \) such that \( f(x + a_n) = f(x + b_n) \) for all \( n \geq N \).

2. **Objective**:
   - Determine if \( f \) must be constant based on these conditions.

3. **Counterexample Construction**:
   - We will construct a function \( f \) that satisfies the given conditions but is not constant.

4. **Construction**:
   - Let \( x = 0.5 \).
   - Choose \( a_n = \frac{1}{n} \) and \( b_n = \frac{1}{2n} \).
   - Define \( f \) as follows:
     \[
     f(t) = 
     \begin{cases} 
     1 & \text{if } t = 0.5 + \frac{1}{n} \text{ or } t = 0.5 + \frac{1}{2n} \text{ for some } n \geq 1, \\
     0 & \text{otherwise}.
     \end{cases}
     \]

5. **Verification**:
   - For \( n \geq 1 \), we have:
     \[
     f\left(0.5 + \frac{1}{n}\right) = 1 \quad \text{and} \quad f\left(0.5 + \frac{1}{2n}\right) = 1.
     \]
   - Therefore, \( f\left(0.5 + \frac{1}{n}\right) = f\left(0.5 + \frac{1}{2n}\right) \) for all \( n \geq 1 \), satisfying the condition \( f(x + a_n) = f(x + b_n) \).

6. **Non-constancy**:
   - However, \( f \) is not constant because:
     \[
     f(0.5) = 0 \quad \text{(since } 0.5 \neq 0.5 + \frac{1}{n} \text{ or } 0.5 + \frac{1}{2n} \text{ for any } n \geq 1).
     \]
   - Hence, \( f \) takes different values at different points, specifically \( f(0.5) = 0 \) and \( f(0.5 + \frac{1}{n}) = 1 \).

7. **Conclusion**:
   - The function \( f \) satisfies the given conditions but is not constant.
   - Therefore, we cannot assert that \( f \) is constant based on the given conditions.

Your task is to write a function `merge_sorted_lists` that merges these two lists into a single sorted list. The function should perform the merge in a single pass through both lists and should not use any built-in sorting functions. 

**Input:**
- Two sorted lists of integers, `list1` and `list2`.

**Output:**
- A single sorted list containing all elements from `list1` and `list2`.

**Sample Input:**
- `list1 = [1, 3, 5]`
- `list2 = [2, 4, 6]`

**Sample Output:**
- `[1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6]`

```python
def merge_sorted_lists(list1, list2):
    """
    Merges two sorted lists into a single sorted list in a single pass.

    Parameters:
    list1 (list): The first sorted list.
    list2 (list): The second sorted list.

    Returns:
    list: A merged sorted list containing all elements from list1 and list2.
    """
    i, j = 0, 0
    merged_list = []
    while i < len(list1) and j < len(list2):
        if list1[i] < list2[j]:
            merged_list.append(list1[i])
            i += 1
        else:
            merged_list.append(list2[j])
            j += 1
    while i < len(list1):
        merged_list.append(list1[i])
        i += 1
    while j < len(list2):
        merged_list.append(list2[j])
        j += 1
    return merged_list

# Example usage
list1 = [1, 3, 5]
list2 = [2, 4, 6]
print(merge_sorted_lists(list1, list2))  # Output: [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6]

Write a function `is_prime(number)` that takes an input `number` and returns `True` if `number` is a prime number, and `False` otherwise. A prime number is defined as a natural number greater than 1 that has no divisors other than 1 and itself. Your function should handle invalid inputs such as negative numbers and non-integers by returning `False`.

**Sample Input and Output:**
- Input: `7`
- Output: `True`
- Input: `10`
- Output: `False`
- Input: `-5`
- Output: `False`
- Input: `2.5`
- Output: `False`

```python
def is_prime(number):
    """
    Determines if the given number is a prime number.
    
    Parameters:
    number (int): The number to check for primality.
    
    Returns:
    bool: True if the number is prime, False otherwise.
    """
    if not isinstance(number, int) or number <= 1:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(number**0.5) + 1):
        if number % i == 0:
            return False
    return True

   213	    def zoom_bounds(self, bounds, zoom_out=1):
   214	        """
   215	        Adapt the zoom to the given bounds. and center the image.
   216	
   217	        Args:
   218	            bounds ([coordinates]): coordinates corners as minx, miny, maxx, maxy in EPSG:4326
   219	            zoom_out (int) (optional): Zoom out the bounding zoom
   220	
   221	        Return:
   222	            self
   223	        """
   224	
   225	        minx, miny, maxx, maxy = bounds
   226	
   227	        # Center map to the centroid of the layer(s)
   228	        self.center = [(maxy - miny) / 2 + miny, (maxx - minx) / 2 + minx]
   229	
   230	        # create the tuples for each corner in (lat/lng) convention
   231	        tl, br, bl, tr = (maxy, minx), (miny, maxx), (miny, minx), (maxy, maxx)
   232	
   233	        # find zoom level to display the biggest diagonal (in km)
   234	        lg, zoom = 40075, 1  # number of displayed km at zoom 1
   235	        maxsize = max(haversine(tl, br), haversine(bl, tr))
   236	        while lg > maxsize:
   237	            (zoom, lg) = (zoom + 1, lg / 2)
   238	
   239	        zoom_out = (zoom - 1) if zoom_out > zoom else zoom_out
   240	
   241	        self.zoom = zoom - zoom_out
   242	
   243	        return self
   244	
   245	    def add_raster(
   246	        self,
   247	        image,
   248	        bands=None,
   249	        layer_name="Layer_" + su.random_string(),
   250	        colormap=plt.cm.inferno,
   251	        x_dim="x",
   252	        y_dim="y",

   255	        get_base_url=lambda _: "https://sepal.io/api/sandbox/jupyter",
   256	        colorbar_position=False,
   257	    ):
   258	        """
   259	        Adds a local raster dataset to the map.
   260	
   261	        Args:
   262	            image (str | pathlib.Path): The image file path.
   263	            bands (int or list, optional): The image bands to use. It can be either a number (e.g., 1) or a list (e.g., [3, 2, 1]). Defaults to None.
   264	            layer_name (str, optional): The layer name to use for the raster. Defaults to None. If a layer is already using this name 3 random letter will be added
   265	            colormap (str, optional): The name of the colormap to use for the raster, such as 'gray' and 'terrain'. More can be found at https://matplotlib.org/3.1.0/tutorials/colors/colormaps.html. Defaults to None.
   266	            x_dim (str, optional): The x dimension. Defaults to 'x'.
   267	            y_dim (str, optional): The y dimension. Defaults to 'y'.
   268	            opacity (float, optional): the opacity of the layer, default 1.0.
   269	            fit_bounds (bool, optional): Wether or not we should fit the map to the image bounds. Default to True.
   270	            get_base_url (callable, optional): A function taking the window URL and returning the base URL to use. It's design to work in the SEPAL environment, you only need to change it if you want to work outside of our platform. See xarray-leaflet lib for more details.
   271	            colorbar_position (str, optional): The position of the colorbar. By default set to False to remove it.
   272	
   273	        Return:
   274	            (LocalTileLayer) the local tile layer embeding the raster member (to be used with other tools of sepal-ui)
   275	        """
   276	
   277	        # force cast to Path
   278	        image = Path(image)
   279	
   280	        if not image.is_file():
   281	            raise Exception(ms.mapping.no_image)
   282	
   283	        # check inputs
   284	        if layer_name in [layer.name for layer in self.layers]:
   285	            layer_name = layer_name + su.random_string()
   286	
   287	        if isinstance(colormap, str):
   288	            colormap = plt.cm.get_cmap(name=colormap)
   289	
   290	        da = rioxarray.open_rasterio(image, masked=True)
   291	
   292	        # The dataset can be too big to hold in memory, so we will chunk it into smaller
   293	        # pieces.
   294	        # That will also improve performances as the generation of a tile can be done
   295	        # in parallel using Dask.
   296	        da = da.chunk((1000, 1000))
   297	
   298	        # unproject if necessary
   299	        epsg_4326 = "EPSG:4326"
   300	        if da.rio.crs != CRS.from_string(epsg_4326):
   301	            da = da.rio.reproject(epsg_4326)
   302	
   303	        multi_band = False
   304	        if len(da.band) > 1 and type(bands) != int:
   305	            multi_band = True
   306	            if not bands:
   307	                bands = [3, 2, 1]
   308	        elif len(da.band) == 1:
   309	            bands = 1
   310	
   311	        if multi_band:
   312	            da = da.rio.write_nodata(0)
   313	        else:
   314	            da = da.rio.write_nodata(np.nan)
   315	        da = da.sel(band=bands)
   316	
   317	        kwargs = {
   318	            "m": self,
   319	            "x_dim": x_dim,
   320	            "y_dim": y_dim,
   321	            "fit_bounds": fit_bounds,
   322	            "get_base_url": get_base_url,
   323	            "colorbar_position": colorbar_position,
   324	            "rgb_dim": "band" if multi_band else None,
   325	            "colormap": None if multi_band else colormap,
   326	        }
   327	
   328	        # display the layer on the map
   329	        layer = da.leaflet.plot(**kwargs)
   330	        layer.name = layer_name
   331	        layer.opacity = opacity if abs(opacity) <= 1.0 else 1.0
   332	
   333	        # add the da to the layer as an extra member for the v_inspector
   334	        layer.raster = str(image)
   335	
   336	        return layer

  <#sep#>You are given a non-negative integer `n`. Your task is to write a function `find_fibonacci(n)` that computes the `n`-th Fibonacci number. The Fibonacci sequence is defined as follows: `F(0) = 0`, `F(1) = 1`, and `F(n) = F(n-1) + F(n-2)` for `n >= 2`. Your function should return the `n`-th Fibonacci number as an integer.

**Sample Input:**
```
7
```

**Sample Output:**
```
13
```

```python
def find_fibonacci(n):
    """
    Computes the n-th Fibonacci number.

    Parameters:
    n (int): A non-negative integer representing the position in the Fibonacci sequence.

    Returns:
    int: The n-th Fibonacci number.
    """
    if n <= 0:
        return 0
    elif n == 1:
        return 1
    else:
        a, b = 0, 1
        for _ in range(2, n + 1):
            a, b = b, a + b
        return b

# Example usage:
# print(find_fibonacci(7))  # Output: 13

Your task is to write a function named `square_list` that computes the square of each integer in the list and returns a new list containing these squared values. The function should handle any list of integers, including empty lists.

**Sample Input:**

```python
def square_list(int_list):
    """
    Computes the square of each integer in the input list and returns a new list with the squared values.

    :param int_list: List of integers to be squared.
    :return: List of squared integers.
    """
    return [x ** 2 for x in int_list]

   <#sep#>You are given a list of integers and a target sum. Your task is to implement a function `find_pairs_with_sum` that identifies all unique pairs of integers from the list that add up to the target sum. The function should return a list of tuples, where each tuple contains a pair of integers that sum to the target. The order of the pairs and the order of integers within each pair does not matter.

**Input:**
- A list of integers `numbers` (1 ≤ len(numbers) ≤ 10^3), where each integer is in the range [-10^3, 10^3].
- An integer `target_sum` (-2 * 10^3 ≤ target_sum ≤ 2 * 10^3).

**Output:**
- A list of tuples, where each tuple contains a pair of integers from the input list that add up to `target_sum`.

**Sample Input:**
```python
numbers = [2, 4, 3, 5, 7, 8, 1]
target_sum = 9
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
[(4, 5), (2, 7), (8, 1)]
```

```python
def find_pairs_with_sum(numbers, target_sum):
    """
    Finds all unique pairs of integers in the list that add up to the target sum.

    :param numbers: List of integers.
    :param target_sum: Integer representing the target sum.
    :return: List of tuples, where each tuple contains a pair of integers that add up to the target sum.
    """
    pairs = []
    seen = set()

    for number in numbers:
        complement = target_sum - number
        if complement in seen:
            pairs.append((complement, number))
        seen.add(number)

    return pairs

- `title`: A string representing the title of the book.
- `author`: A string representing the author of the book.
- `isbn`: A string representing the International Standard Book Number, which uniquely identifies the book.
- `year`: An integer representing the year the book was published.

The class should include the following methods:
- `get_title()`: Returns the title of the book.
- `get_author()`: Returns the author of the book.
- `get_isbn()`: Returns the ISBN of the book.
- `get_year()`: Returns the year the book was published.
- `is_classic()`: Returns `True` if the book was published before the year 1950, otherwise returns `False`.

**Sample Input:**
```
"1984", "George Orwell", "9780451524935", 1949
```

**Sample Output:**
```
1984
George Orwell
9780451524935
1949
True
```

```python
class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, isbn, year):
        """
        Initializes a new Book instance.

        :param title: The title of the book.
        :param author: The author of the book.
        :param isbn: The ISBN of the book.
        :param year: The year the book was published.
        """
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.isbn = isbn
        self.year = year

    def get_title(self):
        """
        Returns the title of the book.

        :return: The title of the book.
        """
        return self.title

    def get_author(self):
        """
        Returns the author of the book.

        :return: The author of the book.
        """
        return self.author

    def get_isbn(self):
        """
        Returns the ISBN of the book.

        :return: The ISBN of the book.
        """
        return self.isbn

    def get_year(self):
        """
        Returns the year the book was published.

        :return: The year the book was published.
        """
        return self.year

    def is_classic(self):
        """
        Determines if the book is a classic (published before 1950).

        :return: True if the book is a classic, False otherwise.
        """
        return self.year < 1950

# Test case
book = Book("1984", "George Orwell", "9780451524935", 1949)
print(book.get_title())   # Output: 1984
print(book.get_author())  # Output: George Orwell
print(book.get_isbn())    # Output: 9780451524935
print(book.get_year())    # Output: 1949
print(book.is_classic())  # Output: True

A palindromic substring reads the same forwards and backwards. For instance, in the string "babad", the longest palindromic substrings are "bab" and "aba", both of which have a length of 3. Write a function `longest_palindrome(s)` that returns the length of the longest palindromic substring.

**Input:**
- A single string `s` (1 ≤ |s| ≤ 1000).

**Output:**
- An integer representing the length of the longest palindromic substring.

**Sample Input:**
```
babad
```

**Sample Output:**
```
3
```

```python
def longest_palindrome(s: str) -> int:
    """
    Returns the length of the longest palindromic substring in the given string s.
    
    :param s: A string consisting of lowercase English letters.
    :return: An integer representing the length of the longest palindromic substring.
    """
    if not s:
        return 0
    
    def expand_around_center(left: int, right: int) -> int:
        """
        Expands around the center indices left and right to find the length of the longest palindromic substring.
        
        :param left: The left index of the center.
        :param right: The right index of the center.
        :return: The length of the longest palindromic substring centered at left and right.
        """
        while left >= 0 and right < len(s) and s[left] == s[right]:
            left -= 1
            right += 1
        return right - left - 1

    start, end = 0, 0
    for i in range(len(s)):
        len1 = expand_around_center(i, i)  # Odd length palindromes
        len2 = expand_around_center(i, i + 1)  # Even length palindromes
        max_len = max(len1, len2)
        if max_len > end - start:
            start = i - (max_len - 1) // 2
            end = i + max_len // 2

    return end - start + 1

Yes. So that's the answer.
</think>To find the sum \(\sum_{k=1}^{100} a_k\) for the sequence defined by \(a_1 = \frac{1}{2}\) and \(a_n = \left( \frac{2n-3}{2n} \right) a_{n-1}\) for \(n \geq 2\), we start by expressing \(a_n\) in a more convenient form.

First, we write the general term \(a_n\) as a product:
\[
a_n = \frac{1}{2} \prod_{m=2}^n \frac{2m-3}{2m}
\]

Next, we simplify the product:
\[
\prod_{m=2}^n \frac{2m-3}{2m} = \frac{1 \cdot 3 \cdot 5 \cdots (2n-3)}{2 \cdot 4 \cdot 6 \cdots (2n)}
\]

The numerator is the product of the first \(n-1\) odd numbers, which can be written as:
\[
1 \cdot 3 \cdot 5 \cdots (2n-3) = \frac{(2n-2)!}{2^{n-1} (n-1)!}
\]

The denominator is the product of the first \(n-1\) even numbers, which can be written as:
\[
2 \cdot 4 \cdot 6 \cdots (2n) = 2^{n-1} (n-1)!
\]

Thus, the product simplifies to:
\[
\prod_{m=2}^n \frac{2m-3}{2m} = \frac{\frac{(2n-2)!}{2^{n-1} (n-1)!}}{2^{n-1} n!} = \frac{(2n-2)!}{2^{2(n-1)} (n-1)! n!}
\]

Therefore, the general term \(a_n\) is:
\[
a_n = \frac{1}{2} \cdot \frac{(2n-2)!}{2^{2(n-1)} (n-1)! n!} = \frac{(2n-2)!}{2^{2n-1} (n-1)! n!}
\]

We recognize that this can be written in terms of the central binomial coefficient:
\[
a_n = \frac{1}{2n} \binom{2(n-1)}{n-1} \cdot \frac{1}{2^{2(n-1)}}
\]

To find the sum \(\sum_{k=1}^{100} a_k\), we note that the sequence \(a_n\) can be expressed as a telescoping series. Define:
\[
c_n = \frac{\binom{2(n-1)}{n-1}}{2^{2(n-1)}}
\]

Then:
\[
a_n = c_n - c_{n+1}
\]

The sum of the first 100 terms is:
\[
\sum_{k=1}^{100} a_k = \sum_{k=1}^{100} (c_k - c_{k+1}) = c_1 - c_{101}
\]

Given \(c_1 = 1\), we have:
\[
c_{101} = \frac{\binom{200}{100}}{2^{200}}
\]

Thus, the sum is:
\[
\sum_{k=1}^{100} a_k = 1 - c_{101} = 1 - \frac{\binom{200}{100}}{2^{200}}
\]

Therefore, the final answer is:
\[
\boxed{1 - \dfrac{\dbinom{200}{100}}{2^{200}}}
\]<#sep#>.

I've uploaded a python code repository in the directory /testbed. Consider the following issue description:

<issue_description>
display a loading statebar when a layer is loading in the map 
## issue 
I experienced some issues with the FCDM module as the map is super slow to load (it uses a adaptative buffer, the more you zoom the slower it gets it's a nightmare). The consequence is that the user clicks frantically on the `zoom` `unzoom` btn and the layer never fully loads and they eventually break the app.

I've looked into the loading widgets that exists in ipyleaflet and that's not very elegant (from my perspective).

## current implementation

I came up with the idea of overwriting the `add_layer` method to add a `StateBar` at the top left corner for every layer and dynamically show them when the tile are reloading. overwritting the `add_layer` method is super versatile as it's the basic function called by all the others (`add_ee_layer`, `addLayer`, `add_raster_layer` ... etc). 

- user doesn't change anything in the way object are added to the map
- each time you add a layer, it adds a statebar writting "loading \<whatever\>"
- the statebare is shown whenever the layer is loading (thank you traitlets)

## questions

- should we remove them when the layer is removed (overwritting remove_layer as well) ?
- should the statebar always be displayed (and instead of changing viz, we could change loading trait) ?
- do you think it could be useful in general ? 

## demo 

Here is a gif demo and the the code I used. 


![feature_demo](https://user-images.githubusercontent.com/12596392/142245503-4b78f635-9700-468c-9202-fa04a13de4b8.gif)

```python
from sepal_ui.mapping import SepalMap
from sepal_ui import sepalwidgets as sw 
from ipyleaflet import WidgetControl
import ee

ee.Initialize()

# create a custom statebar
class StateBar(sw.StateBar):
    
    def __init__(self, **kwargs):
        
        name = kwargs.pop("layer", "layer")
        kwargs["_metadata"] = {"layer": name} 
        
        super().__init__(**kwargs)
        
        self.msg = f"Loading {name}"
        self.loading = True
        
    def activate(self, change):
        
        if change['new']:
            self.show()
        else:
            self.hide()
            
        return

# define a custom Map class
class Map(SepalMap):
    
    layer_state_list = []
    
    def add_layer(self, l):
        
        # call the original function
        super().add_layer(l)
        
        # add a layer state object 
        state = StateBar(layer=l.name)
        self.layer_state_list += [state]
        self.add_control(WidgetControl(widget=state, position='topleft'))
        
        # link it to the layer state
        #layer = next(l for l in self.layers if l.name == name)
        l.observe(state.activate, "loading")
        
        return

# create the map and zoom on congo
test_map = Map()
test_map.zoom =<#sep#>You are given a list of integers `nums` representing the daily stock prices of a company over a period of time. Your task is to implement a function `max_profit(nums)` that calculates the maximum profit that can be achieved by buying and selling the stock on different days. You are allowed to perform only one buy and one sell operation, and the buy operation must occur before the sell operation. If no profit can be made, return 0.

**Constraints:**
- 1 ≤ len(nums) ≤ 10^5
- 0 ≤ nums[i] ≤ 10^4

**Sample Input:**
```
[7, 1, 5, 3, 6, 4]
```

**Sample Output:**
```
5
```

```python
def max_profit(nums):
    """
    Calculate the maximum profit from a single buy and sell operation on stock prices.

    :param nums: List[int] - A list of integers representing daily stock prices.
    :return: int - The maximum profit that can be achieved. Returns 0 if no profit is possible.
    """
    if not nums or len(nums) < 2:
        return 0
    
    min_price = float('inf')
    max_profit = 0
    
    for price in nums:
        if price < min_price:
            min_price = price
        elif price - min_price > max_profit:
            max_profit = price - min_price
    
    return max_profit

# Example usage:
nums = [7, 1, 5, 3, 6, 4]
print(max_profit(nums))  # Output: 5

# load the S2 RGB product (SR)
def maskS2clouds(image):

    qa = image.select('QA60');
    
    # Bits 10 and 11 are clouds and cirrus, respectively.
    cloudBitMask = 1 << 10;
    cirrusBitMask = 1 << 11;

    # Both flags should be set to zero, indicating clear conditions.
    mask = (
        qa
        .bitwiseAnd(cloudBitMask).eq(0)
        .And(qa.bitwiseAnd(cirrusBitMask).eq(0))
    )

    return image.updateMask(mask).divide(10000)

dataset = (
    ee.ImageCollection('COPERNICUS/S2_SR')
    .filterDate('2020-01-01', '2020-01-30')
    .filter(ee.Filter.lt('CLOUDY_PIXEL_PERCENTAGE',20))
    .map(maskS2clouds)
)

visualization = {

  "max": 0.3,
  "bands": ['B4', 'B3', 'B2'],
}

test_map.addLayer(dataset.mean(), visualization, 'RGB')

/test<#sep#>You are given a Python dictionary `data` representing a JSON object with nested structures including dictionaries and lists. Your task is to implement a function `flatten_json(data)` that transforms this nested JSON object into a single-level dictionary. Each key in the resulting dictionary should be a string that represents the path to the value in the original JSON object, with keys separated by dots (`.`). For example, if the original JSON object has a key `name` inside a nested object `person`, the resulting dictionary should have a key `person.name`.

**Input:**
- A Python dictionary `data` representing a JSON object.

**Output:**
- A single-level dictionary with keys representing the path to each value in the original JSON object.

**Sample Input:**
```python
data = {
    "person": {
        "name": "John",
        "address": {
            "city": "New York",
            "zipcode": "10001"
        }
    },
    "hobbies": ["reading", "swimming"],
    "age": 30
}
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
{
    'person.name': 'John',
    'person.address.city': 'New York',
    'person.address.zipcode': '10001',
    'hobbies.0': 'reading',
    'hobbies.1': 'swimming',
    'age': 30
}
```

```python
def flatten_json(data, parent_key='', sep='.'):
    """
    Flattens a nested JSON object into a single-level dictionary.

    :param data: The original JSON object (Python dictionary)
    :param parent_key: The base key string for recursion (default is an empty string)
    :param sep: The separator between keys (default is '.')
    :return: A flattened dictionary
    """
    items = {}
    for k, v in data.items():
        new_key = f"{parent_key}{sep}{k}" if parent_key else k
        if isinstance(v, dict):
            items.update(flatten_json(v, new_key, sep=sep))
        elif isinstance(v, list):
            for i, item in enumerate(v):
                items.update(flatten_json({str(i): item}, new_key, sep=sep))
        else:
            items[new_key] = v
    return items

    66	:code:`sepal_ui` is a lib designed to create elegant python based dashboard in the `SEPAL environment <https://sepal.io/>`__. It is designed on top of the amazing `ipyvuetify <https://ipyvuetify.readthedocs.io/en/latest/introduction.html>`_ library and will help developer to easily create interface for their workflows. 
    67	By using this libraries, you'll ensure a robust and unified interface for your scripts and a easy and complete integration into the SEPAL dashboard of application.

[Current<#sep#>You are given a positive integer `n`. Your task is to write a function `findPrimeFactors` that returns a list of prime factors of `n`. Each prime factor should appear in the list as many times as it divides `n` without leaving a remainder. For example, if `n = 84`, the function should return `[2, 2, 3, 7]` because `84 = 2 * 2 * 3 * 7`.

**Input:**
- A single integer `n` (1 ≤ n ≤ 10^6)

**Output:**
- A list of integers representing the prime factors of `n` in non-decreasing order.

**Sample Input:**
```
84
```

**Sample Output:**
```
[2, 2, 3, 7]
```

```python
def findPrimeFactors(n):
    """
    Returns a list of prime factors of the given integer n.
    Each prime factor appears in the list the number of times it divides n without leaving a remainder.
    
    :param n: Integer, the number to factorize
    :return: List of integers, the prime factors of n
    """
    i = 2
    factors = []
    while i * i <= n:
        if n % i:
            i += 1
        else:
            n //= i
            factors.append(i)
    if n > 1:
        factors.append(n)
    return factors

The calculator will receive a string input representing a mathematical expression consisting of two numbers separated by an operator. The calculator must support the following operators: `+`, `-`, `*`, and `/`. The result of the operation should be returned as a float. If the input string is invalid, the calculator should return an appropriate error message.

**Input:**
- A single string `expression` representing the mathematical operation to be performed. The string will be in the format "num1 operator num2", where `num1` and `num2` are floating-point numbers and `operator` is one of `+`, `-`, `*`, or `/`.

**Output:**
- A float representing the result of the operation, or an error message if the input is invalid.

**Sample Input 1:**

5.6

```python
def calculate(expression):
    """
    Evaluates a simple arithmetic expression provided as a string.
    
    Args:
    expression (str): A string representing the arithmetic operation to be performed.
    
    Returns:
    float or str: The result of the arithmetic operation as a float, or an error message as a string.
    """
    try:
        num1, operator, num2 = expression.split()
        num1 = float(num1)
        num2 = float(num2)
        
        if operator == '+':
            return num1 + num2
        elif operator == '-':
            return num1 - num2
        elif operator == '*':
            return num1 * num2
        elif operator == '/':
            if num2 == 0:
                return "Error: Division by zero"
            return num1 / num2
        else:
            return "Error: Invalid operator"
    except ValueError:
        return "Error: Invalid input format"

=============================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================== 1 failed, 1 passed, 27 warnings in 0.11s =================================================================================================================================================================================================<#sep#>You are given a list of filenames `file_names`. Your task is to write a Python function `count_words_in_files` that reads each file, counts the number of words in each file, and returns a dictionary where the keys are the filenames and the values are the word counts. Words are defined as sequences of alphanumeric characters separated by spaces, punctuation marks, or newline characters. Your function should handle any potential `IOError` exceptions that might occur when trying to open the files. If a file does not exist or cannot be opened, it should be skipped and the function should continue with the remaining files.

**Input:**
- A list of strings `file_names` representing filenames.

**Output:**
- A dictionary where keys are filenames and values are the corresponding word counts.

**Sample Input:**
```python
file_names = ["file1.txt", "file2.txt", "file3.txt"]
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
{"file1.txt": 10, "file2.txt": 15, "file3.txt": 20}
```

```python
import re

def count_words_in_files(file_names):
    """
    Counts the number of words in each file from the given list of filenames.
    
    Parameters:
    file_names (list of str): A list of filenames to be processed.
    
    Returns:
    dict: A dictionary with filenames as keys and their respective word counts as values.
    """
    word_counts = {}
    
    for file_name in file_names:
        try:
            with open(file_name, 'r') as file:
                content = file.read()
                words = re.findall(r'\b\w+\b', content)
                word_counts[file_name] = len(words)
        except IOError:
            continue
    
    return word_counts

# Example usage
file_names = ["file1.txt", "file2.txt", "file3.txt"]
word_counts = count_words_in_files(file_names)
print(word_counts)

</think>To solve the problem, we need to find the product of the distances from \( P' \) to the sides of triangle \( A''B''C'' \), where \( P' \) is the isogonal conjugate of \( P \) and \( A'', B'', C'' \) are the intersections of the lines \( PA', PB', PC' \) with the circumcircle of triangle \( ABC \).

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Isogonal Conjugate and Cyclic Properties:**
   - Given that \( P \) is a point inside triangle \( ABC \) and \( P' \) is its isogonal conjugate, the cevians \( AA', BB', CC' \) intersect at \( P \).
   - The points \( A'', B'', C'' \) are defined as the intersections of the lines \( PA', PB', PC' \) with the circumcircle of \( ABC \).

2. **Cyclic Quadrilateral Properties:**
   - Since \( A'', B'', C'' \) lie on the circumcircle, the quadrilaterals \( AA''A'P \), \( BB''B'P \), and \( CC''C'P \) are cyclic.
   - By the properties of cyclic quadrilaterals, the power of point \( P \) with respect to the circumcircle is the same for all these quadrilaterals:
     \[
     PA \cdot PA'' = PB \cdot PB'' = PC \cdot PC'' = k
     \]
     where \( k \) is the power of point \( P \) with respect to the circumcircle.

3. **Distances from \( P' \) to the Sides of \( A''B''C'' \):**
   - The isogonal conjugate \( P' \) of \( P \) has the property that the distances from \( P' \) to the sides of \( A''B''C'' \) are related to the distances from \( P \) to the sides of \( ABC \).
   - Specifically, the product of the distances from \( P' \) to the sides of \( A''B''C'' \) is the same as the product of the distances from \( P \) to the sides of \( ABC \).

4. **Product of Distances:**
   - The product of the distances from \( P \) to the sides of \( ABC \) is given by the formula involving the area \( \Delta \) of triangle \( ABC \):
     \[
     d_a \cdot d_b \cdot d_c = \frac{8 \Delta^3}{(a b c)^2}
     \]
     where \( d_a, d_b, d_c \) are the distances from \( P \) to the sides \( BC, CA, AB \) respectively, and \( a, b, c \) are the side lengths of \( ABC \).

5. **Isogonal Conjugate and Reciprocal Properties:**
   - Since \( P' \) is the isogonal conjugate of \( P \), the distances from \( P' \) to the sides of \( A''B''C'' \) are reciprocally related to the distances from \( P \) to the sides of \( ABC \).
   - Therefore, the product of the distances from \( P' \) to the sides of \( A''B''C'' \) is the same as the product of the distances from \( P \) to the sides of \( ABC \).

6. **Final Calculation:**
   - Given the properties of isogonal conjugates and the cyclic nature of the quadrilaterals, the product of the distances from \( P' \) to the sides of \( A''B''C'' \) simplifies to:
     \[
     \boxed{1}
     \]

Thus, the product of the distances from \( P' \) to the sides of triangle \( A''B''C'' \) is \(\boxed{1}\).<#sep#> is the probability that no collision has occurred by time t.

   <#sep#>You are given a list of integers `numbers`. Your task is to implement a function `find_gcd` that computes the greatest common divisor (GCD) of all integers in the list. If the list is empty, the function should return `None`. Utilize the `math.gcd` function from the `math` module to determine the GCD of two numbers.

**Input:**
- A list of integers `numbers` (0 ≤ len(numbers) ≤ 1000, 0 ≤ numbers[i] ≤ 10^9).

**Output:**
- An integer representing the GCD of all numbers in the list, or `None` if the list is empty.

**Sample Input:**
```
[48, 64, 80]
```

**Sample Output:**
```
16
```

```python
import math
from functools import reduce

def find_gcd(numbers):
    """
    Computes the greatest common divisor (GCD) of all integers in the list.
    Returns None if the list is empty.
    
    :param numbers: List of integers
    :return: GCD of all integers in the list or None if the list is empty
    """
    if not numbers:
        return None
    return reduce(math.gcd, numbers)

ab = 1000

bd = 1000

cd = (b/2)*(1000/b) = 500

Total = 1000 + 500 + 1000 + 500 = 3000. Same as before.

Wait, here's another thought inspired by events being independent:

Alternatively, note that each pair's contribution can be seen in terms of their probability and collision time, such that the total expectation is the sum over all pairs of the probability that a particular pair is first, times their collision time. So the crux is figuring out, for each pair (i,j), the probability that its collision (if it happens) occurs earlier than all other potential collision times.

The probability that (i,j) are the first is equal to the expectation over all possible direction choices of the indicator function [ the collision time between (i,j) is the smallest among all collision times between any other pairs which do their own collision ] multiplied by the collision time.

Thus, we can model it as:

E = ∑_{all pairs (i,j)} [ E[ collision time of (i,j) * I( (i,j) has the first collision ) ]

The expectation inside the summation can expand to the expectation of the indicator function, scaled by the time, which can be expressed as integrating over possible times where the collision of (i,j) is the earliest among all other colliding pairs.

Computing this expectation would require for each pair the probability that C_{i,j} happens, and that all other C_{k,l} are either not happening or have collision time more than T_ij.

To compute this:

Let T be the collision time between i and j (possibly infinite if they don't collide). The prob P that (i,j) is the first is:

P = P( T_ij < T_{k,l} for all other k,l ).

Thus, the expectation contribution is E[ T_ij * I(T_ij < all other T_{k,l}) }

The problem now reduces to calculating for each pair (i,j):

contrib(i,j) = ∑_{directions of all protons where (i,j) collide} ( probability of choosing those directions for i,j ) × ∏_{k≠i,j, l≠i,j}Prob(elts (k,l) don't collide or collides after T_ij} ) × T_ij

import torch.nn.functional as F
import torch.nn as nn


class ScaledDotProductAttention(nn.Module):
    """ Scaled Dot-Product Attention """

    def __init__(self, temperature, attn_dropout=0.1):
        super().__init__()
        self.temperature = temperature
        self.dropout = nn.Dropout(attn_dropout)

    def forward(self, q, k, v, mask=None):
        """
        q: 256,8,36,64
        k: 256,8,36,64
        v: 256,8,36,64
        mask: 256,1,1,36
        """
        attn = torch.matmul(q / self.temperature, k.transpose(2, 3))
        """
        mask(256,1,1,36)
        attn(256,8,36,36)
        这里用到了tensor的broadcast: 两个tensor必须满足，从最后一个维度开始往前算，维度要么相等，要么为1，要么不存在
        这里的mask中间两个维度为1，可以与attn做broadcast

        将mask的行索引复制到36，得到36×36的mask矩阵，batch中共256个36*36的矩阵，1/256即batch中每个样本的mask再复制到head=8个
        每个batch中样本的mask和各自的互注意力矩阵相乘
        注意力矩阵是36*36是个混淆矩阵，表示第一个元素和其余36个元素的关系，因此mask行列转置无所谓
        """
        if mask is not None:
            attn = attn.masked_fill(mask == 0, -1000000000.0)
        attn = self.dropout(F.softmax(attn, dim=-1))
        output = torch.matmul(attn, v)
        return output, attn


class MultiHeadAttention(nn.Module):
    """ Multi-Head Attention module """

    def __init__(self, n_head, d_model, d_k, d_v, dropout=0.1):
        super().__init__()
        self.n_head = n_head
        self.d_k = d_k
        self.d_v = d_v
        self.w_qs = nn.Linear(d_model, n_head * d_k, bias=False)
        self.w_ks = nn.Linear(d_model, n_head * d_k, bias=False)
        self.w_vs = nn.Linear(d_model, n_head * d_v, bias=False)
        self.fc = nn.Linear(n_head * d_v, d_model, bias=False)
        self.attention = ScaledDotProductAttention(temperature=d_k ** 0.5)
        self.dropout = nn.Dropout(dropout)
        self.layer_norm = nn.LayerNorm(d_model, eps=1e-06)

    def forward(self, q, k, v, mask=None):
        d_k, d_v, n_head = self.d_k, self.d_v, self.n_head
        sz_b, len_q, len_k, len_v = q.size(0), q.size(1), k.size(1), v.size(1)
        residual = q
        q = self.w_qs(q).view(sz_b, len_q, n_head, d_k)
        k = self.w_ks(k).view(sz_b, len_k, n_head, d_k)
        v = self.w_vs(v).view(sz_b, len_v, n_head, d_v)
        q, k, v = q.transpose(1, 2), k.transpose(1, 2), v.transpose(1, 2)
        if mask is not None:
            mask = mask.unsqueeze(1)
        q, attn = self.attention(q, k, v, mask=mask)
        q = q.transpose(1, 2).contiguous().view(sz_b, len_q, -1)
        q = self.dropout(self.fc(q))
        q += residual
        q = self.layer_norm(q)
        return q, attn


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4])
        ]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'n_head': 4, 'd_model': 4, 'd_k': 4, 'd_v': 4}]

Let me check another case where maybe not all constraints are set to maximum. Let's say ab is less than 1000, but maybe other terms can be increased. Maybe if we let some constraints not be tight, but others compensate.

Alternatively, let's think of variables.

Suppose we write variables as follows:

Let’s set a = x, b = y, c = z, d = w.

We have the constraints:

1. xy ≤ 1000

2. xz ≤ 1000

3. yw ≤ 1000

4. zw ≤ 500

We need to maximize xy + xz + yw + zw = (x + w)(y + z)

So, we need to maximize (x + w)(y + z). Let’s think of this as the product of two terms: (x + w) and (y + z). To maximize the product, given the constraints. Maybe we can maximize each term separately, but they are not independent. Let's see.

Let’s try to set up Lagrange multipliers. Maybe that's a method. But since this is a problem likely intended for competitions, maybe there's a smarter way.

Alternatively, let's note that from the constraints:

From 1 and 2: x ≤ 1000/y and x ≤ 1000/z. So, x ≤ min(1000/y, 1000/z)

From 3 and 4: w ≤ 1000/y and w ≤ 500/z. So, w ≤ min(1000/y, 500/z)

Therefore, x + w ≤ min(1000/y, 1000/z) + min(1000/y, 500/z)

Similarly, the expression (x + w)(y + z) is bounded by this.

But this is getting complicated. Maybe instead, consider ratios.

Let’s let’s think in terms of variables.

Let’s suppose that y and z are variables, and we can express x and w in terms of y and z.

From constraints 1 and 2: x ≤ 1000/y and x ≤ 1000/z. Therefore, x ≤ 1000 / max(y, z)

Similarly, from constraints 3 and 4: w ≤ 1000/y and w ≤ 500/z. Therefore, w ≤ min(1000/y, 500/z)

Therefore, x + w ≤ 1000 / max(y, z) + min(1000/y, 500/z)

Then, (x + w)(y + z) ≤ [1000 / max(y, z) + min(1000/y, 500/z)] * (y + z)

This is quite a complex expression. Maybe we can split into cases based on the relationship between y and z.

Case 1: y ≥ z

Then, max(y, z) = y. So, x ≤ 1000/y

min(1000/y, 500/z) = If y ≥ z, then 500/z ≤ 500/y (since z ≤ y). But 1000/y vs 500/z. Since z ≤ y, 500/z ≥ 500/y. Therefore, comparing 1000/y and 500/z: which is smaller?

Wait, z ≤ y, so 500/z ≥ 500/y. So, 1000/y vs 500/z. Depending on the relationship between z and y/2. Let's see:

If 500/z ≤ 1000/y → 500/z ≤ 1000/y → y ≤ 2z

But since y ≥ z, then y is between z and 2z.

So, if z ≤ y ≤ 2z, then 500/z ≥ 1000/y → min(1000/y, 500/z) = 1000/y

Wait, wait. Let me check:

If y ≤ 2z, then 1000/y ≥ 500/z because:

1000/y ≥ 500/z → 1000z ≥ 500y → 2z ≥ y. Which is exactly the condition. So, in this case, when y ≤ 2z, then min(1000/y, 500/z) = 500/z. Wait, no:

Wait, no. If we have two numbers A and B, min(A, B) is the smaller one. If A = 1000/y and B = 500/z. Then:

If 1000/y ≤ 500/z → 1000z ≤ 500y → 2z ≤ y.

But in the case where y ≤ 2z, the opposite is true: 2z ≥ y → 1000z ≥ 500y → 1000/y ≥ 500/z → So, min(1000/y, 500/z) = 500/z if 1000/y ≥ 500/z, which happens when y ≤ 2z. Wait, this is confusing. Let me take concrete examples.

Suppose y = z. Then, 1000/y and 500/z = 1000/y and 500/y. So, min is 500/y. So, in this case, when y = z, it's 500/y.

If y = 2z, then 1000/y = 1000/(2z) = 500/z, which is equal to 500/z. So, min(500/z, 500/z) = 500/z.

If y is between z and 2z, for example, y = 1.5z, then 1000/y = 1000/(1.5z) ≈ 666.67/z, and 500/z. So, min is 500/z.

If y > 2z, say y = 3z, then 1000/y = 1000/(3z) ≈ 333.33/z, and 500/z. So, min is 333.33/z.

Therefore, for Case 1 (y ≥ z):

If y ≤ 2z, then min(1000/y, 500/z) = 500/z

If y > 2z, then min(1000/y, 500/z) = 1000/y

Therefore, splitting into two subcases:

Subcase 1a: y ≥ z and y ≤ 2z

Then, (x + w)(y + z) ≤ (1000/y + 500/z)(y + z) = 1000 + 1000*(z/y) + 500*(y/z) + 500

Simplify: 1500 + 1000*(z/y) + 500*(y/z)

To maximize this expression. Let me denote t = y/z. Since y ≥ z and y ≤ 2z, then t ∈ [1, 2].

So, substituting t: y = tz, so z/y = 1/t, y/z = t

Therefore, the expression becomes:

1500 + 1000*(1/t) + 500*t

So, we need to maximize 1500 + (1000/t) + 500t for t ∈ [1, 2]

Take derivative with respect to t: derivative is -1000/t² + 500

Set derivative to zero: -1000/t² + 500 = 0 → 500 = 1000/t² → t² = 2 → t = sqrt(2) ≈ 1.414. Which is within [1, 2].

Therefore, maximum at t = sqrt(2). Let's compute the value:

1500 + 1000/(sqrt(2)) + 500*sqrt(2)

Convert to decimal:

1000/sqrt(2) ≈ 707.11

500*sqrt(2) ≈ 707.11

Total: 1500 + 707.11 + 707.11 ≈ 1500 + 1414.22 ≈ 2914.22

   214	        """
   215	        Adapt the zoom to the given bounds. and center the image.
   216	
   217	        Args:
   218	            bounds ([coordinates]): coordinates corners as minx, miny, maxx, maxy in EPSG:4326
   219	            zoom_out (int) (optional): Zoom out the bounding zoom
   220	
   221	        Return:
   222	            self
   223	        """
   224	
   225	        minx, miny, maxx, maxy = bounds
   226	
   227	        # Center map to the centroid of the layer(s)
   228	        self.center = [(maxy - miny) / 2 + miny, (maxx - minx) / 2 + minx]
   229	
   230	        # create the tuples for each corner in (lat/lng) convention
   231	        tl, br, bl, tr = (maxy, minx), (miny, maxx), (miny, minx), (maxy, maxx)
   232	
   233	        # find zoom level to display the biggest diagonal (in km)
   234	        lg, zoom = 40075, 1  # number of displayed km at zoom 1
   235	        maxsize = max(haversine(tl, br), haversine(bl, tr))
   236	        while lg > maxsize:
   237	            (zoom, lg) = (zoom + 1, lg / 2)
   238	
   239	        zoom_out = (zoom - 1) if zoom_out > zoom else zoom_out
   240	
   241	        self.zoom = zoom - zoom_out
   242	
   243	        return self

import torch.nn.functional as F
import torch.nn as nn


class ScaledDotProductAttention(nn.Module):
    """ Scaled Dot-Product Attention """

    def __init__(self, temperature, attn_dropout=0.1):
        super().__init__()
        self.temperature = temperature
        self.dropout = nn.Dropout(attn_dropout)

    def forward(self, q, k, v, mask=None):
        """
        q: 256,8,36,64
        k: 256,8,36,64
        v: 256,8,36,64
        mask: 256,1,1,36
        """
        attn = torch.matmul(q / self.temperature, k.transpose(2, 3))
        """
        mask(256,1,1,36)
        attn(256,8,36,36)
        这里用到了tensor的broadcast: 两个tensor必须满足，从最后一个维度开始往前算，维度要么相等，要么为1，要么不存在
        这里的mask中间两个维度为1，可以与attn做broadcast

        将mask的行索引复制到36，得到36×36的mask矩阵，batch中共256个36*36的矩阵，1/256即batch中每个样本的mask再复制到head=8个
        每个batch中样本的mask和各自的互注意力矩阵相乘
        注意力矩阵是36*36是个混淆矩阵，表示第一个元素和其余36个元素的关系，因此mask行列转置无所谓
        """
        if mask is not None:
            attn = attn.masked_fill(mask == 0, -1000000000.0)
        attn = self.dropout(F.softmax(attn, dim=-1))
        output = torch.matmul(attn, v)
        return output, attn


class MultiHeadAttention(nn.Module):
    """ Multi-Head Attention module """

    def __init__(self, n_head, d_model, d_k, d_v, dropout=0.1):
        super().__init__()
        self.n_head = n_head
        self.d_k = d_k
        self.d_v = d_v
        self.w_qs = nn.Linear(d_model, n_head * d_k, bias=False)
        self.w_ks = nn.Linear(d_model, n_head * d_k, bias=False)
        self.w_vs = nn.Linear(d_model, n_head * d_v, bias=False)
        self.fc = nn.Linear(n_head * d_v, d_model, bias=False)
        self.attention = ScaledDotProductAttention(temperature=d_k ** 0.5)
        self.dropout = nn.Dropout(dropout)
        self.layer_norm = nn.LayerNorm(d_model, eps=1e-06)

    def forward(self, q, k, v, mask=None):
        d_k, d_v, n_head = self.d_k, self.d_v, self.n_head
        sz_b, len_q, len_k, len_v = q.size(0), q.size(1), k.size(1), v.size(1)
        residual = q
        q = self.w_qs(q).view(sz_b, len_q, n_head, d_k)
        k = self.w_ks(k).view(sz_b, len_k, n_head, d_k)
        v = self.w_vs(v).view(sz_b, len_v, n_head, d_v)
        q, k, v = q.transpose(1, 2), k.transpose(1, 2), v.transpose(1, 2)
        if mask is not None:
            mask = mask.unsqueeze(1)
        q, attn = self.attention(q, k, v, mask=mask)
        q = q.transpose(1, 2).contiguous().view(sz_b, len_q, -1)
        q = self.dropout(self.fc(q))
        q += residual
        q = self.layer_norm(q)
        return q, attn


class PositionwiseFeedForward(nn.Module):
    """ A two-feed-forward-layer module """

    def __init__(self, d_in, d_hid, dropout=0.1):
        super().__init__()
        self.w_1 = nn.Linear(d_in, d_hid)
        self.w_2 = nn.Linear(d_hid, d_in)
        self.layer_norm = nn.LayerNorm(d_in, eps=1e-06)
        self.dropout = nn.Dropout(dropout)

    def forward(self, x):
        residual = x
        x = self.w_2(F.relu(self.w_1(x)))
        x = self.dropout(x)
        x += residual
        x = self.layer_norm(x)
        return x


class EncoderLayer(nn.Module):
    """ Compose with two layers """

    def __init__(self, d_model, d_inner, n_head, d_k, d_v, dropout=0.1):
        super(EncoderLayer, self).__init__()
        self.slf_attn = MultiHeadAttention(n_head, d_model, d_k, d_v,
            dropout=dropout)
        self.pos_ffn = PositionwiseFeedForward(d_model, d_inner, dropout=
            dropout)

    def forward(self, enc_input, slf_attn_mask=None):
        enc_output, enc_slf_attn = self.slf_attn(enc_input, enc_input,
            enc_input, mask=slf_attn_mask)
        enc_output = self.pos_ffn(enc_output)
        return enc_output, enc_slf_attn


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'d_model': 4, 'd_inner': 4, 'n_head': 4, 'd_k': 4, 'd_v': 4}]

Subcase 1b: y > 2z

Case 2: z > y

Similarly, min(1000/y, 500/z). Let's see:

Since z > y, so 500/z < 500/y

Compare 1000/y and 500/z. If 1000/y ≤ 500/z → 1000z ≤ 500y → 2z ≤ y. But since z > y, this can't happen. Therefore, 1000/y > 500/z, so min(1000/y, 500/z) = 500/z.

Thus, x + w ≤ 1000/z + 500/z = 1500/z

Then, (x + w)(y + z) ≤ (1500/z)(y + z)

But since z > y, y + z < 2z, so (1500/z)(y + z) < 1500/z * 2z = 3000

So, again, less than 3000.

Therefore, the maximum in the case where z > y is less than 3000.

So, combining these cases, the maximum seems to be 3000, which is achieved when y = z, and certain other conditions. Wait, in the first case where we set ab=1000, ac=1000, which forced b=c, and then bd=1000 and cd=500. But that led to a contradiction unless we adjust variables properly.

Wait, in the first case where we set ab=1000, ac=1000, that makes b=c. Then, if b=c, then from cd=500, we get d=500/c=500/b. From bd=1000, we have d=1000/b. Therefore, 500/b=1000/b ⇒ 500=1000, which is impossible. But earlier when we did the substitution, we must have made a mistake.

Wait, let me check again. Suppose we set ab=1000 and ac=1000. Then, from ab=1000, a=1000/b. From ac=1000, a=1000/c. Therefore, 1000/b=1000/c ⇒ b=c.

Then, substituting into the other constraints. From bd ≤ 1000, with d being a variable. Similarly, cd ≤ 500. Since c=b, then d ≤ 500/b.

But also, a=1000/b, d=500/b.

So, the variables are a=1000/b, d=500/b, c=b, and b is variable. Then, check bd: b*d = b*(500/b) = 500 ≤ 1000? Yes. Similarly, cd = c*d = b*(500/b) = 500 ≤ 500. So, that's okay.

So, then ab + ac + bd + cd = 1000 + 1000 + 500 + 500 = 3000. So, all constraints are satisfied here. Wait, but if we set ab=1000, ac=1000, and then set d=500/b, which allows bd=500 and cd=500, but the constraint bd ≤ 1000 is satisfied (since 500 ≤ 1000). So, this works.

Therefore, in this case, we have ab=1000, ac=1000, bd=500, cd=500. So, the maximum is 3000.

Alternatively, another case where ab=1000, bd=1000, and cd=500. Let's see. If ab=1000, then a=1000/b. If bd=1000, then d=1000/b. If cd=500, then c=500/d=500/(1000/b)= (500b)/1000= b/2. Therefore, c= b/2. Then, check ac: a*c= (1000/b)*(b/2)= 500 ≤ 1000, which is okay. So, in this case, ab=1000, ac=500, bd=1000, cd=500. So, total is 1000 + 500 + 1000 + 500= 3000. Same result.

Therefore, regardless of how we set the constraints (either ab and ac to 1000, or ab and bd to 1000 and cd to 500), the maximum total is 3000. Therefore, the maximum possible value is 3000.

But let's check if there's another way. Suppose that we set three of the constraints to their maximum. For example, ab=1000, ac=1000, bd=1000. Then, as before, this would require b=c, d=1000/b, a=1000/b. Then cd= c*d= b*(1000/b)=1000, which violates the cd ≤500 constraint. So, that's not possible. Therefore, we can't set three of them to their maximum.

Alternatively, if we set ab=1000, ac=1000, cd=500. Then, similar to before, with b=c, d=500/c=500/b. Then, bd= b*(500/b)=500 ≤1000, which is okay. So, in that case, only two constraints are at maximum, others are below. But the total is still 3000.

So, in all cases, it seems that 3000 is the maximum possible. Therefore, the answer should be 3000.

**Final Answer**
\boxed{3000}
</think>To find the maximum possible value of \(ab + ac + bd + cd\) given the constraints \(ab \le 1000\), \(ac \le 1000\), \(bd \le 1000\), and \(cd \le 500\), we start by factoring the expression:

\[
ab + ac + bd + cd = (a + d)(b + c)
\]

Our goal is to maximize \((a + d)(b + c)\) under the given constraints.

### Case 1: \(ab = 1000\) and \(ac = 1000\)

<#sep#>import math
import torch
import torch.nn as nn
from numpy import prod


def getLayerNormalizationFactor(x):
    """
    Get He's constant for the given layer
    https://www.cv-foundation.org/openaccess/content_iccv_2015/papers/He_Delving_Deep_into_ICCV_2015_paper.pdf
    """
    size = x.weight.size()
    fan_in = prod(size[1:])
    return math.sqrt(2.0 / fan_in)


class ConstrainedLayer(nn.Module):
    """
    A handy refactor that allows the user to:
    - initialize one layer's bias to zero
    - apply He's initialization at runtime
    """

    def __init__(self, module, equalized=True, lrMul=1.0, initBiasToZero=True):
        """
        equalized (bool): if true, the layer's weight should evolve within
                         the range (-1, 1)
        initBiasToZero (bool): if true, bias will be initialized to zero
        """
        super(ConstrainedLayer, self).__init__()
        self.module = module
        self.equalized = equalized
        if initBiasToZero:
            self.module.bias.data.fill_(0)
        if self.equalized:
            self.module.weight.data.normal_(0, 1)
            self.module.weight.data /= lrMul
            self.weight = getLayerNormalizationFactor(self.module) * lrMul

    def forward(self, x):
        x = self.module(x)
        if self.equalized:
            x *= self.weight
        return x


class EqualizedLinear(ConstrainedLayer):

    def __init__(self, nChannelsPrevious, nChannels, bias=True, **kwargs):
        """
        A nn.Linear module with specific constraints
        Args:
            nChannelsPrevious (int): number of channels in the previous layer
            nChannels (int): number of channels of the current layer
            bias (bool): with bias ?
        """
        ConstrainedLayer.__init__(self, nn.Linear(nChannelsPrevious,
            nChannels, bias=bias), **kwargs)


class AdaIN(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, dimIn, dimOut, epsilon=1e-08):
        super(AdaIN, self).__init__()
        self.epsilon = epsilon
        self.styleModulator = EqualizedLinear(dimIn, 2 * dimOut, equalized=
            True, initBiasToZero=True)
        self.dimOut = dimOut

    def forward(self, x, y):
        batchSize, nChannel, _width, _height = x.size()
        tmpX = x.view(batchSize, nChannel, -1)
        mux = tmpX.mean(dim=2).view(batchSize, nChannel, 1, 1)
        varx = torch.clamp((tmpX * tmpX).mean(dim=2).view(batchSize,
            nChannel, 1, 1) - mux * mux, min=0)
        varx = torch.rsqrt(varx + self.epsilon)
        x = (x - mux) * varx
        styleY = self.styleModulator(y)
        yA = styleY[:, :self.dimOut].view(batchSize, self.dimOut, 1, 1)
        yB = styleY[:, self.dimOut:].view(batchSize, self.dimOut, 1, 1)
        return yA * x + yB


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'dimIn': 4, 'dimOut': 4}]

Their contribution to the expectation is q_ij multiplied by the probability that所有 other pairs either don’t collide or have their own collision time > t_ij, multiplied by t_ij. 

Hence, the contribution for pair (i,j) would be:

E_i_j = q_ij * P( no other collision happens before time t_ij | (i,j) collides at time t_ij) * t_ij

However, perhaps for large N, the collision between other pairs can be considered as independent events and thus multiply their probabilities. 

If we assume that independence (~which might not hold), then:

The probability that no other pairs' collision happens before t_ij 

is the product over all other pairs (k,l) not equal to (i,j) 

 of the probability that pair (k,l) either doesn't collide, or if they do, their collision time > t_ij. 

Hence, 

P(no other collision before t_ij) 

= product_{(k,l) != (i,j)} [ P(k,l不相撞 OR k,l 相撞时间 > t_ij) ]

Hence, the contribution E_i_j would be:

 probability_direction_pair[i,j's collision] ( q_ij ) * 

 product_{ 其他 pair (k,l) } [ 

　　　P(k,l don't collide) + P(k,l do collide with T_{kl} > T_{ij}) 

　 ]

 multiplied by t_ij.

This is the concise formula.

Thus, the total expectation is the sum over all pairs' contributions summed as such.

Note that for pairs other than (i,j), their contribution to the product is:

for pair (k,l), the factor is:

1 - Q_{k,l}(t_{ij)},

where Q_{k,l}(t) is the probability of (k,l) collide and their collision time ≤ t_{ij)} 

But t_{ij} is the collision time of (i,j) when they choose to collide.

Hence, for each pair (i,j), and for each possible directions that may force a collision ( i.e., collision time T_ {ij} ), we can imagine the product over the remaining pairs of factors that depend on their own collision probabilities and whether their possible collision times are before T_ij.

import torch.nn.functional as F
import torch.nn as nn


class ScaledDotProductAttention(nn.Module):
    """ Scaled Dot-Product Attention """

    def __init__(self, temperature, attn_dropout=0.1):
        super().__init__()
        self.temperature = temperature
        self.dropout = nn.Dropout(attn_dropout)

    def forward(self, q, k, v, mask=None):
        """
        q: 256,8,36,64
        k: 256,8,36,64
        v: 256,8,36,64
        mask: 256,1,1,36
        """
        attn = torch.matmul(q / self.temperature, k.transpose(2, 3))
        """
        mask(256,1,1,36)
        attn(256,8,36,36)
        这里用到了tensor的broadcast: 两个tensor必须满足，从最后一个维度开始往前算，维度要么相等，要么为1，要么不存在
        这里的mask中间两个维度为1，可以与attn做broadcast

        将mask的行索引复制到36，得到36×36的mask矩阵，batch中共256个36*36的矩阵，1/256即batch中每个样本的mask再复制到head=8个
        每个batch中样本的mask和各自的互注意力矩阵相乘
        注意力矩阵是36*36是个混淆矩阵，表示第一个元素和其余36个元素的关系，因此mask行列转置无所谓
        """
        if mask is not None:
            attn = attn.masked_fill(mask == 0, -1000000000.0)
        attn = self.dropout(F.softmax(attn, dim=-1))
        output = torch.matmul(attn, v)
        return output, attn


class MultiHeadAttention(nn.Module):
    """ Multi-Head Attention module """

    def __init__(self, n_head, d_model, d_k, d_v, dropout=0.1):
        super().__init__()
        self.n_head = n_head
        self.d_k = d_k
        self.d_v = d_v
        self.w_qs = nn.Linear(d_model, n_head * d_k, bias=False)
        self.w_ks = nn.Linear(d_model, n_head * d_k, bias=False)
        self.w_vs = nn.Linear(d_model, n_head * d_v, bias=False)
        self.fc = nn.Linear(n_head * d_v, d_model, bias=False)
        self.attention = ScaledDotProductAttention(temperature=d_k ** 0.5)
        self.dropout = nn.Dropout(dropout)
        self.layer_norm = nn.LayerNorm(d_model, eps=1e-06)

    def forward(self, q, k, v, mask=None):
        d_k, d_v, n_head = self.d_k, self.d_v, self.n_head
        sz_b, len_q, len_k, len_v = q.size(0), q.size(1), k.size(1), v.size(1)
        residual = q
        q = self.w_qs(q).view(sz_b, len_q, n_head, d_k)
        k = self.w_ks(k).view(sz_b, len_k, n_head, d_k)
        v = self.w_vs(v).view(sz_b, len_v, n_head, d_v)
        q, k, v = q.transpose(1, 2), k.transpose(1, 2), v.transpose(1, 2)
        if mask is not None:
            mask = mask.unsqueeze(1)
        q, attn = self.attention(q, k, v, mask=mask)
        q = q.transpose(1, 2).contiguous().view(sz_b, len_q, -1)
        q = self.dropout(self.fc(q))
        q += residual
        q = self.layer_norm(q)
        return q, attn


class PositionwiseFeedForward(nn.Module):
    """ A two-feed-forward-layer module """

    def __init__(self, d_in, d_hid, dropout=0.1):
        super().__init__()
        self.w_1 = nn.Linear(d_in, d_hid)
        self.w_2 = nn.Linear(d_hid, d_in)
        self.layer_norm = nn.LayerNorm(d_in, eps=1e-06)
        self.dropout = nn.Dropout(dropout)

    def forward(self, x):
        residual = x
        x = self.w_2(F.relu(self.w_1(x)))
        x = self.dropout(x)
        x += residual
        x = self.layer_norm(x)
        return x


class DecoderLayer(nn.Module):
    """ Compose with three layers """

    def __init__(self, d_model, d_inner, n_head, d_k, d_v, dropout=0.1):
        super(DecoderLayer, self).__init__()
        self.slf_attn = MultiHeadAttention(n_head, d_model, d_k, d_v,
            dropout=dropout)
        self.enc_attn = MultiHeadAttention(n_head, d_model, d_k, d_v,
            dropout=dropout)
        self.pos_ffn = PositionwiseFeedForward(d_model, d_inner, dropout=
            dropout)

    def forward(self, dec_input, enc_output, slf_attn_mask=None,
        dec_enc_attn_mask=None):
        dec_output, dec_slf_attn = self.slf_attn(dec_input, dec_input,
            dec_input, mask=slf_attn_mask)
        dec_output, dec_enc_attn = self.enc_attn(dec_output, enc_output,
            enc_output, mask=dec_enc_attn_mask)
        dec_output = self.pos_ffn(dec_output)
        return dec_output, dec_slf_attn, dec_enc_attn


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'d_model': 4, 'd_inner': 4, 'n_head': 4, 'd_k': 4, 'd_v': 4}]

import torch.nn as nn


class Attention(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, heads, n_state):
        super().__init__()
        assert n_state % heads == 0
        self.heads = heads
        self.n_state = n_state
        self.depth = self.n_state // self.heads

    def split_heads(self, x: 'torch.Tensor', batch: 'int', seq_len: 'int'):
        x = x.reshape((batch, seq_len, self.heads, self.depth))
        return x.permute(0, 2, 1, 3)

    def combine_heads(self, x: 'torch.Tensor', batch: 'int', seq_len: 'int'):
        x = x.permute(0, 2, 1, 3)
        return x.reshape((batch, seq_len, self.n_state))


class Conv1d(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, nf, nx, stdev=0.02):
        super().__init__()
        self.nf = nf
        self.nx = nx
        self.stdev = stdev
        self.w = nn.Parameter(torch.normal(size=[1, self.nx, self.nf], mean
            =0.0, std=self.stdev))
        self.b = nn.Parameter(torch.zeros([self.nf]))

    def forward(self, x: 'torch.Tensor'):
        shape = x.size()
        start, nx = shape[:-1], shape[-1]
        return torch.reshape(torch.matmul(torch.reshape(x, [-1, nx]), torch
            .reshape(self.w, [-1, self.nf])) + self.b, start + (self.nf,))


class RelativeAttention(Attention):

    def __init__(self, heads, n_state, max_sequence):
        super().__init__(heads, n_state)
        self.max_sequence = max_sequence
        self.c_attn = Conv1d(self.n_state * 3, self.n_state)
        self.c_proj = Conv1d(self.n_state, self.n_state)
        self.E = nn.Parameter(torch.Tensor(self.heads, self.max_sequence, 
            n_state // heads))
        nn.init.xavier_normal_(self.E)

    def relative_attn(self, q: 'torch.Tensor', E: 'torch.Tensor', batch:
        'int', seq_len: 'int'):
        q_ = q.permute(1, 0, 2, 3)
        q_ = q_.reshape(self.heads, batch * seq_len, self.depth)
        E = E[:, self.max_sequence - seq_len:]
        rel = q_ @ E.transpose(-1, -2)
        rel = rel.reshape(self.heads, batch, seq_len, seq_len)
        rel = torch.nn.functional.pad(rel, (1, 0), 'constant', 0)
        rel = rel.reshape(self.heads, batch, seq_len + 1, seq_len)
        rel = rel[:, :, 1:]
        rel = rel.permute(1, 0, 2, 3)
        return rel

    def multihead_attn(self, q: 'torch.Tensor', k: 'torch.Tensor', v:
        'torch.Tensor', batch, seq_len, mask=None):
        w = q @ k.transpose(-1, -2)
        w = w + self.relative_attn(q, self.E, batch, seq_len)
        w = w * (1 / self.depth ** (1 / 2))
        if mask is not None:
            w += mask
        w = w.softmax(-1)
        a = w @ v
        return a

    def forward(self, x: 'torch.Tensor', mask=None):
        batch, seq_len, _ = x.size()
        c = self.c_attn(x)
        q, k, v = torch.split(c, self.n_state, dim=2)
        q = self.split_heads(q, batch, seq_len)
        k = self.split_heads(k, batch, seq_len)
        v = self.split_heads(v, batch, seq_len)
        a = self.multihead_attn(q, k, v, batch, seq_len, mask)
        a = self.combine_heads(a, batch, seq_len)
        a = self.c_proj(a)
        return a


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'heads': 4, 'n_state': 4, 'max_sequence': 4}]

from torch import nn
import torch.nn.functional as F


class AdaptiveInstanceNorm2d(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, num_features, eps=1e-05, momentum=0.1):
        super(AdaptiveInstanceNorm2d, self).__init__()
        self.num_features = num_features
        self.eps = eps
        self.momentum = momentum
        self.weight = None
        self.bias = None
        self.register_buffer('running_mean', torch.zeros(num_features))
        self.register_buffer('running_var', torch.ones(num_features))

    def forward(self, x):
        assert self.weight is not None and self.bias is not None, 'Please assign weight and bias before calling AdaIN!'
        b, c = x.size(0), x.size(1)
        running_mean = self.running_mean.repeat(b)
        running_var = self.running_var.repeat(b)
        x_reshaped = x.contiguous().view(1, b * c, *x.size()[2:])
        out = F.batch_norm(x_reshaped, running_mean, running_var, self.
            weight, self.bias, True, self.momentum, self.eps)
        return out.view(b, c, *x.size()[2:])

    def __repr__(self):
        return self.__class__.__name__ + '(' + str(self.num_features) + ')'


class LayerNorm(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, num_features, eps=1e-05, affine=True):
        super(LayerNorm, self).__init__()
        self.num_features = num_features
        self.affine = affine
        self.eps = eps
        if self.affine:
            self.gamma = nn.Parameter(torch.Tensor(num_features).uniform_())
            self.beta = nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(num_features))

    def forward(self, x):
        shape = [-1] + [1] * (x.dim() - 1)
        mean = x.view(x.size(0), -1).mean(1).view(*shape)
        std = x.view(x.size(0), -1).std(1).view(*shape)
        x = (x - mean) / (std + self.eps)
        if self.affine:
            shape = [1, -1] + [1] * (x.dim() - 2)
            x = x * self.gamma.view(*shape) + self.beta.view(*shape)
        return x


class Conv2dBlock(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, input_dim, output_dim, kernel_size, stride, padding=
        0, norm='none', activation='relu', pad_type='zero'):
        super(Conv2dBlock, self).__init__()
        self.use_bias = True
        if pad_type == 'reflect':
            self.pad = nn.ReflectionPad2d(padding)
        elif pad_type == 'replicate':
            self.pad = nn.ReplicationPad2d(padding)
        elif pad_type == 'zero':
            self.pad = nn.ZeroPad2d(padding)
        else:
            assert 0, 'Unsupported padding type: {}'.format(pad_type)
        norm_dim = output_dim
        if norm == 'bn':
            self.norm = nn.BatchNorm2d(norm_dim)
        elif norm == 'in':
            self.norm = nn.InstanceNorm2d(norm_dim)
        elif norm == 'ln':
            self.norm = LayerNorm(norm_dim)
        elif norm == 'adain':
            self.norm = AdaptiveInstanceNorm2d(norm_dim)
        elif norm == 'none':
            self.norm = None
        else:
            assert 0, 'Unsupported normalization: {}'.format(norm)
        if activation == 'relu':
            self.activation = nn.ReLU(inplace=True)
        elif activation == 'lrelu':
            self.activation = nn.LeakyReLU(0.2, inplace=True)
        elif activation == 'prelu':
            self.activation = nn.PReLU()
        elif activation == 'selu':
            self.activation = nn.SELU(inplace=True)
        elif activation == 'tanh':
            self.activation = nn.Tanh()
        elif activation == 'none':
            self.activation = None
        else:
            assert 0, 'Unsupported activation: {}'.format(activation)
        self.conv = nn.Conv2d(input_dim, output_dim, kernel_size, stride,
            bias=self.use_bias)

    def forward(self, x):
        x = self.conv(self.pad(x))
        if self.norm:
            x = self.norm(x)
        if self.activation:
            x = self.activation(x)
        return x


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'input_dim': 4, 'output_dim': 4, 'kernel_size': 4,
        'stride': 1}]

gpt: <think> Okay, let's see. The problem is to find the shortest closed route that goes through every edge at least once. Oh right, that's called an Euler tour, but wait, maybe not exactly. Because an Euler tour requires traversing each edge exactly once. But here the problem says "at least once". Wait the question says "the route must go through every edge at least once". Hmm, so this is like a route that covers all edges, possibly traversing some multiple times, but the goal is to minimize the total distance. So this is known as the "Route inspection problem" or the Chinese Postman Problem (CPP).

Right, the Chinese Postman Problem! So the solution involves finding the minimal total distance such that every edge is traversed at least once. The standard approach is: 

First, check if the graph is Eulerian. An Eulerian graph (for an undirected graph) is one where all vertices have even degree. If that's the case, then the shortest route is just the sum of all edge weights, since you can form an Eulerian circuit which traverses each edge exactly once. So that's easy then.

But if the graph isn't Eulerian, that means there are vertices with odd degrees. For the Chinese Postman Problem, the solution requires pairing up these odd-degree vertices so that we can duplicate the minimal paths between each pair, which adds up to the minimal extra distance needed to make all degrees even. Then the total distance is the sum of all edges plus the minimal added edges.

Wait, so the steps are:

1. Find all vertices with odd degree.

2. The number of such vertices must be even because the sum of degrees is even.

3. Pair them up into (k/2) pairs, where k is the number of odd-degree vertices. Then find the shortest path between each pair and duplicate those paths. The total minimal distance to add is the sum of the shortest paths of these pairs, choosing the pairing that gives the minimal total.

4. Then the answer is the original total edge sum plus the minimal added distance from step 3.

So the main challenge here is computing the minimal matching for the shortest paths between all pairs of odd-degree vertices.

So first, I need to:

- Compute all nodes' degrees.

- Collect all the odd-degree nodes.

- Then compute the shortest paths between all pairs of those nodes.

- Then find the minimal weight matching for these pairs, which pairs them optimally so that each is in exactly one pair and the total distance is minimal.

Okay, so the first thing is to process the input, construct the adjacency list or matrix, then calculate the degree for each node.

Wait, the edges are weighted and undirected, so each edge contributes to two nodes' degrees. Well, even if there are multiple edges between the same nodes, each edge is counted in the degrees. So for example, if two nodes have three edges between them, each node's degree is increased by three, right?

Right, so each edge's two endpoints get their degree incremented by one each. So the first step is straightforward.

Alternatively, let's structure this.

Suppose we have the list of odd nodes: O = {v1, v2,...,vm}. We need to pair them up into m/2 pairs such that the sum of the shortest paths between the pairs is as small as possible.

This is exactly the minimum weight perfect matching problem for a graph where the edge weights between each pair of nodes is the shortest distance between them. So we can model this problem as a complete graph where each pair of odd nodes is connected with an edge of weight equal to the shortest path between them. The goal is to find a perfect matching in this complete graph where each node is in exactly one edge, and the sum of edge weights is minimal.

Therefore, the problem reduces to computing the minimum weight perfect matching on the complete graph formed by the odd nodes with edge weights as the shortest paths between them.

For m nodes where m is even, and the number of nodes could be up to 14 (since |V| is up to 15, maybe 14 odd nodes), the m can be 14.

The minimum weight perfect matching can be found using the Blossom algorithm (Edmonds' algorithm), which is O(n^3). For 14 nodes, 14^3 is manageable.

    75	The Conventional Commits specification is a lightweight convention on top of commit messages. It provides an easy set of rules for creating an explicit commit history; which makes it easier to write automated tools on top of. This convention dovetails with SemVer, by describing the features, fixes, and breaking changes made in commit messages.

    79	What can I push and where
    80	-------------------------
    81	
    82	Our branching system embed some rules to avoid crash of the production environment. If you want to contribute to this framework, here are some basic rules that we try our best to follow :
    83	
    84	-   the modification you offer is solving a critical bug in prod : **PR in hotfix**
    85	-   the modification you propose solve the following issues : test, documentation, typo, quality, refactoring, translation **PR in master**
    86	-   the modification you propose is a new feature : open an issue to discuss with the maintainers and then **PR to develop**
    87	
    88	the maintainers will try their best to use PR for new features, to help the community follow the development, for other modification they will simply push to the appropriate branch

<#sep#>import math
import torch
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.nn.functional as F


class Attention(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, embed_dim, hidden_dim=None, out_dim=None, n_head=1,
        score_function='dot_product', dropout=0):
        """ Attention Mechanism
        :param embed_dim:
        :param hidden_dim:
        :param out_dim:
        :param n_head: num of head (Multi-Head Attention)
        :param score_function: scaled_dot_product / mlp (concat) / bi_linear (general dot)
        :return (?, q_len, out_dim,)
        """
        super(Attention, self).__init__()
        if hidden_dim is None:
            hidden_dim = embed_dim // n_head
        if out_dim is None:
            out_dim = embed_dim
        self.embed_dim = embed_dim
        self.hidden_dim = hidden_dim
        self.n_head = n_head
        self.score_function = score_function
        self.w_k = nn.Linear(embed_dim, n_head * hidden_dim)
        self.w_q = nn.Linear(embed_dim, n_head * hidden_dim)
        self.proj = nn.Linear(n_head * hidden_dim, out_dim)
        self.dropout = nn.Dropout(dropout)
        if score_function == 'mlp':
            self.weight = nn.Parameter(torch.Tensor(hidden_dim * 2))
        elif self.score_function == 'bi_linear':
            self.weight = nn.Parameter(torch.Tensor(hidden_dim, hidden_dim))
        else:
            self.register_parameter('weight', None)
        self.reset_parameters()

    def reset_parameters(self):
        stdv = 1.0 / math.sqrt(self.hidden_dim)
        if self.weight is not None:
            self.weight.data.uniform_(-stdv, stdv)

    def forward(self, k, q):
        if len(q.shape) == 2:
            q = torch.unsqueeze(q, dim=1)
        if len(k.shape) == 2:
            k = torch.unsqueeze(k, dim=1)
        mb_size = k.shape[0]
        k_len = k.shape[1]
        q_len = q.shape[1]
        kx = self.w_k(k).view(mb_size, k_len, self.n_head, self.hidden_dim)
        kx = kx.permute(2, 0, 1, 3).contiguous().view(-1, k_len, self.
            hidden_dim)
        qx = self.w_q(q).view(mb_size, q_len, self.n_head, self.hidden_dim)
        qx = qx.permute(2, 0, 1, 3).contiguous().view(-1, q_len, self.
            hidden_dim)
        if self.score_function == 'dot_product':
            kt = kx.permute(0, 2, 1)
            score = torch.bmm(qx, kt)
        elif self.score_function == 'scaled_dot_product':
            kt = kx.permute(0, 2, 1)
            qkt = torch.bmm(qx, kt)
            score = torch.div(qkt, math.sqrt(self.hidden_dim))
        elif self.score_function == 'mlp':
            kxx = torch.unsqueeze(kx, dim=1).expand(-1, q_len, -1, -1)
            qxx = torch.unsqueeze(qx, dim=2).expand(-1, -1, k_len, -1)
            kq = torch.cat((kxx, qxx), dim=-1)
            score = torch.tanh(torch.matmul(kq, self.weight))
        elif self.score_function == 'bi_linear':
            qw = torch.matmul(qx, self.weight)
            kt = kx.permute(0, 2, 1)
            score = torch.bmm(qw, kt)
        else:
            raise RuntimeError('invalid score_function')
        score = F.softmax(score, dim=0)
        output = torch.bmm(score, kx)
        output = torch.cat(torch.split(output, mb_size, dim=0), dim=-1)
        output = self.proj(output)
        output = self.dropout(output)
        return output, score


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 1, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 1, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'embed_dim': 4}]
<#sep#>/x). Then:

[ x + b ]/[x b ] = f( x + b )

Left side: (x +b )/(x b ) =1/b +1/x

So:

1/b +1/x =f(x + b )

But b =f(1/x), so:

Initialize a distance matrix dist with dimensions V x V.

First, set all dist[i][j] to infinity except dist[i][i] = 0.

Then for each edge (s, t, d):

- for each such edge, if d is smaller than the current dist[s][t], set dist[s][t] = min(dist[s][t], d)

- also set dist[t][s] = same as above.

Once all edges are processed, then run Floyd-Warshall to compute all-pairs shortest paths.
